People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Um… yeah, hi. I guess I’m here because some things I’ve been doing lately are starting to look kind of strange to other people. It’s not like I’m trying to cause problems or anything.

I know that probably sounds weird. I just… I figured I should talk to someone before it gets taken the wrong way.

At least I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with development or learning when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I got through school okay — high school and everything. I wasn’t in special classes or anything like that, as far as I remember.\n\nMaybe I wasn’t the best student all the time, but I don’t think there was anything major development-wise. My parents never really said there was a problem. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as lining receipts or tapping the calculator, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like that. I’m not thinking, like, “If I don’t tap it nine times, something bad will happen.”\n\nIt’s more that I just start doing it, and then later I realize it didn’t really make sense. Like the receipts on the floor, or standing by the copier with my hands up… I didn’t have some reason in my head. I know it probably sounds odd. I’m trying to explain it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress, but they keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry about stuff like anybody, but I don’t think I have thoughts that I’m fighting with or trying to push away and they keep forcing themselves back.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean… but I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: When that energy comes on, I cannot sit through a normal bookkeeping shift without pacing, opening extra tabs, reorganizing files, or gone several nights sleeping only two or three hours and still felt ready to go to work and class, like sleep was just taking time away from getting everything figured out.\n- key: S039\n  name: software, art equipment, and a deposit for a trip to meet studio owners because at the time it felt like the obvious next step and I did not really think about rent or my card balance.\n- key: description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Looking back, the wired, overconfident or irritable ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Kind of… but I don’t really think of it like a ritual, if that makes sense. It’s more like I just start doing it, and then I feel like I should finish it a certain way.

Like with the receipts, I’ll line them up across the floor, or with the calculator I’ll tap it in sets—usually nine. I know that probably sounds odd. And sometimes I’ve just stood by the copier with my hands up, and I don’t really have a good reason until someone asks if I’m okay.

I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. It just happens, and then afterward I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if I’d say “overwhelming” exactly, because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but once I start, it does feel like I need to keep going until it feels finished.

software, art equipment, and a deposit for a trip to meet studio owners because at the time it felt like the obvious next step and I did not really think about rent or my card balance.\n- key: ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, yeah… I think that’s part of it. Like with the receipts, lining them across the floor does make it feel more organized somehow, even if I know it’s not really something I’m supposed to be doing.

And the calculator tapping in nines, I don’t know if it’s symmetry exactly, but it feels like there’s a right way to finish it. If I stop wrong, it just feels off.

I know it probably sounds strange. I’m not trying to cause problems at work or school. It’s just these things I’ve started doing, and then I feel embarrassed when someone notices—like with the copier, standing there with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay. Yeah… I think orderly is probably the closest word for it. Not like I’m trying to make everything perfect, but once I start, it feels like there’s a way it’s supposed to be.

At least I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with development or learning when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I got through school okay — high school and everything. I wasn’t in special classes or anything like that, as far as I remember.\n\nMaybe I wasn’t the best student all the time, but I don’t think there was anything major development-wise. My parents never really said there was a problem. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as lining receipts or tapping the calculator, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like that. I’m not thinking, like, “If I don’t tap it nine times, something bad will happen.”\n\nIt’s more that I just start doing it, and then later I realize it didn’t really make sense. Like the receipts on the floor, or standing by the copier with my hands up… I didn’t have some reason in my head. I know it probably sounds odd. I’m trying to explain it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress, but they keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry about stuff like anybody, but I don’t think I have thoughts that I’m fighting with or trying to push away and they keep forcing themselves back.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean… but I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you can't easily get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I might get stuck thinking about work or whether I did something wrong, but not in the way where it feels like some thought is invading my head and I can’t get rid of it.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. I’m trying to answer it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to make friends or understand what others are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I’m not the most outgoing person, but I can get along with people okay, especially at work when there’s a clear reason to talk.\n\nSometimes I might not know exactly what someone means, but I think that’s normal? I haven’t really thought of it as not understanding people’s feelings. If anything, I just try to be polite and not cause problems. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unintentionally saying or doing something that offends or upsets others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. I mean, I’m sure I’ve said things the wrong way before, like everybody does, but I don’t usually have people telling me I offended them or anything.\n\nIf someone seems upset, I try to back off or apologize. I don’t want to come across rude. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding when someone is being sarcastic or joking?\n\nPatient: Not usually, I don’t think. Like, if someone is really dry about it, I might miss it for a second, but most of the time I can tell when people are joking.\n\nI guess I just try to read the situation and laugh if it seems like that’s what they mean. I haven’t had people point it out as a problem or anything. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as lining receipts or tapping the calculator, are part of a routine or ritual that you follow?\n\nPatient: Kind of… but I don’t really think of it like a ritual, if that makes sense. It’s more like I just start doing it, and then I feel like I should finish it a certain way.\n\nLike with the receipts, I’ll line them up across the floor, or with the calculator I’ll tap it in sets—usually nine. I know that probably sounds odd. And sometimes I’ve just stood by the copier with my hands up, and I don’t really have a good reason until someone asks if I’m okay.\n\nI’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. I usually just try to keep it normal and not make anyone uncomfortable. Then I realize how strange it probably looks. Does that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as lining receipts or tapping the calculator, are making things feel more orderly or symmetrical for you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah… I think that’s part of it. If I stop wrong, it just feels off.\n\nI know it probably sounds strange. I’m not trying to cause problems at work or school. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think “uncomfortable” is probably the right word. I don’t know that something terrible would happen, exactly, but if I can’t finish it the way it feels like it’s supposed to go, it just feels really wrong.

That’s kind of why I came in. I’ve started doing these things at work and school that don’t make a lot of sense when I say them out loud—like lining receipts across the floor, tapping the calculator in sets of nine, or standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay.

I know it sounds strange. I’m not trying to make a scene or anything. It just feels hard to stop until it’s… done right, I guess. Is that answering what you mean?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure there’s one specific thought, exactly. It’s more like… I start doing it and it feels like I’m supposed to keep going that way.

Like I said, that’s kind of why I came in—because I’ve been doing things at work and school that don’t really make sense when I try to explain them. Lining receipts across the floor, tapping the calculator in sets of nine, or standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay.

I know that probably sounds weird. I don’t mean it to be a problem. I just… I can tell it isn’t normal, and I wasn’t sure if I should be worried about it.
